ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e96	It is only to note that the world's attention in these days is probably not focused on the budget debates in the Grand Duchy. They are aimed at Copenhagen and the important negotiations that are taking place there. World leaders should, during the ten days they and their delegations sit together, reach a binding agreement on CO2 savings. It will require historical courage and political competence. And if they succeed, it's a very good news for the world. At the same time, it is a victory for knowledge and for research. A climate agreement in Copenhagen is impossible to imagine without science. The entire Copenhagen Summit is based on scientific research. We have knowledge of climate change from thousands of scientific research projects. These have been carried out by researchers from many countries, including Norwegians. Norway is a relatively large climate research nation. In the case of scientific publications, we are the number. 15 on a global ranking. Per capita, we're actually the biggest. We have very good professional environments in several fields, in particular in the field of marine modelling, regional climate scenarios and polar research. One example is the Mountain Centre. They have recently demonstrated that the sea’s ability to absorb CO2 is reduced. That means that more of the greenhouse gases we emit remain in the atmosphere. This reinforces the greenhouse effect. This is a very good example of why it has been right to invest in research — basic research as a mission research. But we need to know more. This is why the 2010 budget for research into the environment and climate will increase. The mountain centre is one of the budget winners. But science is not enough in Copenhagen. The models for different negotiated solutions have been developed by community suites. Professor Arild Underdal, who I've had the pleasure of having as a head of state science, has developed contract systems for complicated negotiations. Economic theory has researched models for quota systems. Cultural knowledge and linguistic skills are also necessary if the world is to agree. Anyway, what we're seeing now is that our collective knowledge, the accumulated human beings through generations, can and will be used to save the world from dramatic changes we don't overlook the consequences of. Most people living today are surrounded by knowledge. We talk about new technology, in smarter ways of solving problems, in a technologically developed cultural and entertainment world, in a medical development that allows us to live longer and move over long distances in a short time, through how we communicate. Developing all possibilities will require knowledge. But it also requires knowledge to use the possibilities. Our future will be based on knowledge development and not least our ability to share this knowledge. The entire Norwegian school system must be, and should be, a place where each student and student is given the opportunity to develop and their abilities in a safe environment. We have to bet on knowledge without going beyond well-being, and we have to bet on safe school environments without going beyond learning. In this budget, increased focus continues on quality in the training cycle. The debate in recent years has increasingly been about knowledge and quality in school. It is entirely in line with the Workers' Party's history of education policy. It was with Gudmund Hernes' entry into the social debate, gradually in the role of Minister of Church, Education and Research, that the reform phase began. The New Year's Eve 1986 Hernes wrote a chronicle in Dagbladet with a clear message: Norwegian education does not have good enough quality. And he claimed that the lack of quality had nothing to do with appropriations, as the usual perception was. Since then, reforms in primary, secondary and higher education have been in line. The raising of knowledge is the last reform. It has been necessary, but now the reforms need to be settled. It means yes to a reform break, but not no to development. That is why we are continuing the strong commitment to knowledge and research, as announced in the Red-Green Government platform. Yesterday, the Statistical Centre was able to present new figures showing an increase in the number of Nordics completing higher education. Record-lave 30 pst. the population today has only primary education. Norway has a high level of education, a high level of research and is the OECD country with the highest proportion of the workforce in occupations requiring training. Our challenge is to ensure high quality in all parts of the education system, to provide all access to education and to ensure skills for an increasingly knowledge-based working life. I want to highlight three issues in particular: continuing to focus on quality in primary education, systematically working to reduce the drop-out rate in high school and to prepare for increased student growth in the years to come. If these objectives are to be achieved, early action is essential. The strong bet on kindergartens has been right. Once again, the number of hours goes up in primary school. In addition, we are now introducing free homework from the 1st to the 4th step. We know that social differences affect children's school outcomes. Not all parents are able to help with homework, and not all parents give homework to their own children. That is why this new initiative is very important. Everyone agrees that the dropout in secondary education is worrying and too high. Poor results from high school affect non-response, which in turn increases the risk of disability and exclusion of young people from working life. The main question all the principals have to ask is, what is my school doing wrong, which makes the students stop? In many places, active efforts are being made to get more people to complete their study or professional skills, but in many places it is too little. We need to work out measures that work. We need to spread knowledge about this to all schools. We have to make sure that every young person experiences that he or she is being taken seriously. In this year's budget, we're adding 58 million. kr to measures to increase the efficiency of implementation. It's a very good contribution, but it won't be enough. The causes of high rates of non-response are complex and require many measures in several areas. That is why the Committee's work on St.meld will be. No 44 for 2008-2009, Education Line, next year a very important work. We don't know how many more students there will be in the years to come, but we know that there will be more. This means that we need to have good and stable funding for the university and higher education sectors. We have to take advantage of the entire sector as it is developed today. Several students have to take all or part of the education out. We donate money to several places of study in this year's budget, and it will have to go on. In any case, high quality has to be a primary goal, even though there are more students. Then we need more robust professional environments. We're going to stimulate it by 50 million. kr for increased cooperation, work sharing and innovation. There has been a sharp increase in research and development in recent years. This continues in next year's budget. Next year we'll spend about $23.6 billion, which is 0.97 pst. of gross domestic product. I have been involved in climate research. There is a focus on upgrading equipment, which is a good thing for the research community. Regional research funds also receive an increase. The budget for higher education and the technical education sectors is DKK 24.5 billion, a growth of 4 pst. Several places of study, several student homes and increased efforts at cooperation are areas I want to highlight. The Norwegian Church plays a significant role in society. The church is more than a seremoniarena, and it has an engagement beyond fighting around female bishops and gay priests. That's good. A few days ago, the Church’s organisations handed over 34 000 signatures to Minister of Development Erik Solheim, who wanted a fair climate agreement in Copenhagen. The head of the Council of Churches, Jens-Petter Johnsen, carried an irresistible sheet. The symbolism was not to be misunderstood. The Old Testament tells us about the Flood, sent by God. Climate destruction can lead to a new deluge, but this time created by ourselves, by men. The Church has an important moral voice. The fact that it chooses to use it is correct — especially to apply it to existing conditions, such as environmental threats. I will also praise the Church for the work it does in terms of dialogue across religions. In a multicultural society — as Norway has become — it is very important. The State budget for the year is characterised by a record use of revenues from the petroleum sector. We spend the next year 44.6 billion dollars more than expected returns in 2010. That's right, in a situation where the financial crisis has led to demand failure. Nevertheless, this year we have also heard the opposition and interest groups call for more money as a solution to all problems. Then I will again draw out Gudmund Hernes. In 1986 he wrote about the low quality of teaching at universities: ‘In Norway it is common to start with the economy. [[...]] Major appropriations for basic research, equipment and not least study funding would help. But to see it all as a question of allocation obscures the problems." In the years that come after this budget, we will most likely have to tighten up on the use of money. Solving the most important tasks in a better way — without spending even more money — will be the most important task for society. As politicians, we must dry up to take the lead on that very challenge. I am convinced that this is possible, but then the path of thought in many sectors, including education and research, needs to be changed. I will end by thanking the Cooperation Committee and referring to the position of the Church, Education and Research Committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e227	This is the main debate in the budget harvest of the year. I just want to say right away that I experience it as a privilege to participate in this important debate. The importance of the debate is also illustrated by the fact that five state councils have reported their presence here today. I think the government should have honor for that. I have to admit that I thought I had left the climate debate after four years in the Energy and Environment Committee, but I did not. There we often discussed what to live on from the 10–15–20 next years. What we are discussing today is how to maintain the standard of living and welfare of a post-petroleum economy. The management of human capital, or brain power, which is a more popular concept, in Norwegian society is the topic of today's debate. The brainpower, as everyone knows, is the largest single factor in the Norwegian national wealth. I'm going to talk more about higher education and research, but first a little bit about basic education. About bullying: It's like a lot of kids get scared every day to go to school – thousands of kids do it every day. It is a pity that there had to be three Swedish State Councils before the government took this seriously. I am pleased that after Mette Hanekamhaug of the Progress Party and Trine Skei Grande of the Left challenged state council, the answer has actually been clear and clear: bullying is unacceptable whether the bully is adult or child, and the fight for a bully-free school is to be intensified. It is also the case that the strongest students are forgotten too often. Norwegian law gives all primary school students equal rights to adapted education regardless of whether they are more or less theoretically strong than the average. Unfortunately, the idea of similarity and socialist thinking makes this right constantly suppressed. Out in the real world, out in the Norwegian school, there are too few people who get organized to teach at a higher level than their classmates. This is sin and something paradoxical. There are not many football runners who would disagree that if you don't get to play football with the best possible coaches and players before you get 19, you can't inspire to become a footballer. Most football talents also debut at the junior team while they are still boys players, without this fact starting a major debate. I also remind you of 14-year-old Moiko, as State Councillor Halvorsen and I have discussed oral Question Time earlier, and who points out in the evening post on 17 November that the public school forced her to mediocrity. Not giving talented children and young people the opportunity to realise their full academic potential as early as possible has lost brain power. Socialistic thinking also means that resistance to private actors in the field of education is high. I do not belong to those who believe that private schools can or should dominate Norwegian basic education. However, national research and statistics show that private schools have a better culture for learning, better results and more satisfied pupils and parents. But then the same results also point out that the reasons for these findings are that the private are measured on results, compete on quality and have to put the user in the center. Measuring, competition and the user at the centre could also be achieved without opening up to private actors, if this was an objective in itself, but it requires free school choice. The practice in Norwegian municipalities is that the redder they are, the less free choice of school is. Then I'll be right in front of the Church. The church agreement shows that we've initiated a process where we're going to separate church and state. Eight out of ten Nordics are members of the Norwegian Church. We are now in the largest reform process the Church has ever faced. Then it would have been desirable if the Church could have used its resources to focus on the big change process that is going on, and not on fighting for the day-to-day operation. Unfortunately, it now looks as if the Church's last year as part of the state administration will be characterized by undisputed performances, trade fairs and decays for church buildings. So to higher education and research. There is often a lot of talk in the public room about the wave of old age, but unfortunately not so much about the wave of youth and the wave of students. A combination of larger youth schools and increased study propensity will increase the number of those who will at any time be in higher education, or at least be qualified for and wish for higher education, by between 20,000 and 100,000 in the next years. The real figure is likely to be between 50,000 and 80 000 by 2014. Some people make this almost as a problem. Of course it isn't. This is the gift package of the times for a Norwegian government. The central government alone needs an estimated 20,000 new high school graduates each year for the next few years. Municipalities need thousands of engineers and other training groups for municipal administration. If health reform is to have some kind of chance of success, municipalities need a massive number of qualified health professionals. Even before any legislation on teacher density in primary school, the school also needs far more teachers. And – a little daunting – one also takes into account the seemingly insatiable need for communication advisors in the departments, it is clear that the need for higher education labour in the public sector by 2025 only means that we have to hope that the projection of study propensity ends in the upper part of the estimate, between 80,000 and 100 000. This is a budget debate. I have noted that the government, with the Aasland State Council at its head, has claimed that this year's budget is a very good research budget. I can stretch to agree in part. Research is growing usefully over some budget chapters. Research on renewable energy deserves some credit. On two points, however, I would say that the Government is also failing in the field of research. The overall budget rhetoric has been that it is a budget for the provision of competitive industry. I have some challenges in general to see what point in the national budget at all this has been addressed. In the field of research, at least industry exposed to competition is not protected. The FUNN tax scheme will not be strengthened and more or less meaningless restrictions will be continued in 2010. Worse still, in what may be a crisis year for the competitive industry and the technical and industrial institutes, the User-led Innovation Arena, the BIA, will not have the means to launch a single new project. This is the first time since the scheme was established. The statement to the Council of State Giske, who is not going to be present today, has been that the Research Council within the budget of the BIA has so far spent too much money. As far as I understand, the Research Council's mission in the context of the financial crisis package was to maintain the activities of the projects under way. This has been largely achieved by the Research Council, for which they should be given the credit. But the government says that too much money has been spent in 2009. Then I want to challenge the government, one of the five councils, to tell me which projects were implemented in 2009, which should have been scrapped to release funds for new projects in the crisis year 2010. The second point where the government is failing the research is in the UH sector. The government assumes that it will be approx. 6 000 more students than in 2009. The student-based and results-based part of the appropriations is increasing in line with this. But that's how part of the basic appropriations for universities are also intended to go to education. But there can be no doubt that, in terms of total appropriations for higher education, no matter how much polemics you take into account the use of concepts around what is in a complex funding system, or how many samples you drop, the result of student growth and lack of funding is that universities need to give priority between maintaining the level of research or the quality of studies. Knowing that a large part of basic research takes place in the hands of universities, and that today's students are future researchers, this also helps to weaken the impression of the government's budget proposal as a good research budget. No matter what individual study sites choose to prioritise, research or study quality, this is a waste of brain power and human capital. The same applies to the question of student funding, which we have discussed many times in Stortinget. All parties promised 10 pst. increase in study funding. In the 2010 budget, there is no increase in purchasing power for the students. In practice, this means that no matter what standard is used, Norwegian students will have a purchasing power that places them in the category ‘poor’. Nevertheless, students with parents with good finances will be able to focus fully on education, while students with parents with a more general economy will have to be behind the 7-Eleven case when they should rather be at the reading hall, library or lecture. This too is wasted brain power and human capital, and it contributes to the impression that the Norwegian education system reinforces social differences in society. We agree on a lot. I look forward to the debate, and insofar as cooperation between the magnificence and government will also take place in the next four-year period. I take up the proposals of the Progress Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e988	The right to freedom of belief and life is a fundamental human right. Freedom of belief and life means that the individual has the right to believe what he or she wants, to express and to live in harmony with his or her faith, to change his or her faith, and to have a view of life that involves not having any religious faith. The fact that we live in a society where people have different religions is a question that addresses our entire welfare society. The school has done a lot in recent years to take care of the diversity of life that exists among their students. Hospitals have increasingly taken into account the fact that people there have different religions. Not too long ago, I gave a prize to a lady who had worked with several different nationalities in the care of dementia. Here we are not good enough in all areas of our welfare society, which should increasingly take into account that people have different religions. It's about respect for each one. In the Soria Moria Declaration, we are just calling for a contribution to the creation of more life-synthesis neutral serums. It's not so easy for anyone who loses their love and who may not belong to any community of life at all, that there's no room where you can have a last moment with their very closest. We say that the welfare society should take care of us from cradle to grave, but we are not good enough to take care of the people of a society characterized by many religions, as it is today. In the Oslo area is now approx. 8 pst. of the population members of religious and life views other than the Norwegian Church. And about half of the municipalities only have church buildings that are suitable for burial rituals, even if you do not belong in the Norwegian Church. So here we have to make a lot of effort in terms of digging, and also in other areas of our welfare community. The task of the State is to contribute to strengthening and protecting freedom of belief and freedom of life. In Norway, the country has an objective of pursuing an active, supportive belief and life-view policy and of actively promoting religious and life-seeking activities. Norway is in a special position to provide financial support to the religious and life views. We have a grant scheme that ensures a high level of economic equality for all religious and life-seeking communities in Norway. In 2008, it was approx. 400,000 eligible members of religious and life views outside the Norwegian Church – there will be grants to more than 600 religious and life views. More than half of these are members of Christian religious communities. The Catholic Church and the Pentecostal movement are the two largest groups, approx. 20 pst. are members of Islamic religious communities and 19 p.m. are members of life-vision societies. In the attitude of the state and the Norwegian Church, the Stortinget points to the need for an overall belief and life-view policy, and that such a policy must primarily affect the various practical aspects of the functioning of the religious and life-view societies in Norway. In accordance with the position of the Grand Government, the Government will present a case of belief and life-view policy in dialogue with the religious and life-view societies, with a view to creating a comprehensive policy in this area. The Ministry of Culture and the Church, which changes its name if not for too long, administers a grant scheme for privately owned church buildings. The aim is to give religious communities outside the Norwegian Church, private foundations and voluntary organisations within the Norwegian Church the opportunity to build church buildings with lower self-financing. The name of the grant scheme may seem somewhat misleading, as “church buildings” are primarily associated with the Christian faith, but the arrangement thus includes construction, or parts of building, which a religious community uses to exercise its religious faith. In practice, the scheme has helped to finance a wide variety of religious buildings — from small prayer houses to large mosques. For the Government, it is important to contribute to dialogue and cooperation between the religious and life views communities in Norway. Openness and conversation promote mutual understanding and contribute to respect for inequality and common values on which our society is to build.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e1040	Following the adoption of the broad church agreement in Stortinget on 10 April 2008, the Norwegian Church is facing the greatest change in the framework of the state church system since 1814. The agreement implies that we are on our way to a distinction between state and church, so that the Church itself appoints bishops and proselytes. The church must still be a church of the people and have a special position over the state. Over a thousand years, the Church has been involved in expressing Norwegian culture, history and social life in good and bad. On the other side of 80 pst. of the inhabitants are members of the Church. In church acts such as baptism, confirmation, weddings and funerals, it turns out that the Church is used by a large majority, which in turn helps to maintain the Norwegian Church as a popular church. In today's multicultural societies, knowledge of one's own faith, religious identity, values and traditions is very important for new generations. Understanding and being safe on one's own identity may give greater understanding to those who have a different belief or outlook on life. With the significance of the Church for both active and more sporadic churchgoers, it is therefore of great importance that the majority government realises that the reform must be followed up with the necessary financial appropriations to facilitate the success of church reform. The objective of maintaining the spiritual service of the Church of Norway in all congregations shall be to present the service in all communities. Appropriations for the Government, inter alia: performance is too scarce and therefore this objective cannot be achieved. The Norwegian Church is faced with major challenges in terms of performance, both of keeping priests in service, of recruiting new job vacancies and of setting up more posts to meet the high demand of more performers. I would like to remind you of the background of the precarious situation in which the pastor's ministry is. In the last ten years, two major reforms have been carried out to improve the working situation of the clergy. In 2001, the so-called Friday Agreement was implemented. It meant that the priests had reduced their working time from six days to five days a week, and should thus have almost equal working time to other workers. This agreement meant that the service was reduced by 17 p.m. and gave a reduction of approx. 233 years' work for in-service workers. New provisions were introduced for the provision of the services in 2004. This meant that the services were reorganised, so that the priests were subsequently employed in the prostitutes and not, as in the past, in the past. The courts were largely given administrative tasks. It was these new provisions that later got the name proste reform. The reform resulted in 106 pros being halved in active working hours, representing a reduction of 53 performers. During the same period that the Church has lost working time corresponding to 286 performance positions, it has been added to approx. 100 new annual shifts in the service, i.e. a reduction of 186 jobs. This does not take government parties into account in their budget. Instead, it is said that there are 100 more priests now than it was ten years ago. This is certainly true, but it was before these reforms were carried out in relation to working time and the proste reform. Proste reform is now considered to be carried out in all dioceses, and the leadership of the prosthetics is strengthened in accordance with the reform. Several national and regional meetings have been held for the prosthetics, and in this way the reform is described as positive. But the purpose of the reform, which was to improve the working day of the priests so that they could join wider and closer working communities in addition to being able to specialise, is not feasible. The large amount of work on the individual pastor and reduced appropriations for the performance over several years has led to a lack of performance, making it impossible for the pastors to participate in the offer offered by the proste reform. In its note on the proste reform, the majority, consisting of the government parties, say: ‘By means of the proste reform, better management and cooperation between the prosecuting priests have been facilitated. The majority believe that further efforts must be made in the future to exploit the opportunities offered by the reform, inter alia, with a view to strengthening the follow-up and management of the individual pastor.» If the parties to the government see that follow-up and management are to be carried out within the financial framework with which they benefit from the performance in this and in previous years' budgets, there will in any case be even less time for the pastor to engage in active church activities. Since the parties to the Government did not wish to grant the necessary means of compensating for these reduced posts, the availability to individuals has been reduced. Church acts such as funerals are not always carried out under the law, and anyone can well imagine what extra burden this entails for those who have lost one of their loved ones. Thus, the reform has not improved the working situation of performers. In the context of a question I asked the Minister of the Church, who was concerned about whether she was comfortable with the development of the Norwegian Church, where we experience the cancellation of religious services and that other church activity has been reduced because of the lack of wage resources, the minister pointed out in his reply up to several times the importance of the Church's having budgetary discipline. The Progress Party is also concerned that both the Church and others should respect budgets. But unlike the Government, the Progress Party is a realist and assumes that in principle enough money will be allocated to solve the legal duties and carry out the activity imposed on the Church. When the Government states that all congregations in the Norwegian Church are to have permanent spiritual service so that the service is present in all communities, and it does not, however, take into account that the number of performance positions is reduced by 186 positions, the Government may not have understood the problem facing the Church and its members, considering that there is a need for the priest's services both in the Church and in family matters. At the hearings held by the Committee, both the Union and several different organisations expressed concern at the lack of performance. It's not useful to grant 2 or 10 million. to recruitment measures where there is no money available on the ordinary salary or temporary budget to pay the benefits. A cross of thought is also that the formidable pressure exerted by the clergy leads to the cessation of many and others. The party of progress is committed to the content of the church settlement and sees the importance of maintaining the Church of Norway as a people's church, where the priest still has an important role to play. I therefore refer to the alternative budget of the Progress Party, where we propose to increase the appropriations for the services by 30 million. kr. This amount will not meet the real need, but is the amount for which a person expects to be able to hire during 2010. We can constantly read about churches that are failing — roofstones fall down, and at all events many of the cultural treasures, as the churches are, are in a sad constitution. The municipalities are constantly told that the government has increased the municipal budgets so much that they are given priority to maintaining and renovating the churches out of their own resources. The same argument meets municipal politicians when other tasks are to be solved. For the Progress Party, church buildings are an important part of our national values and our culture. Therefore, the Progress Party proposes to strengthen the post Grant for church activities in the municipalities by 30 million. kr. These measures do not need further dissemination and information – these are actions to be launched now to help these children now. One in five students leaves high school without being able to read and write well enough to function properly in the business world. One in three drops out of high school. He will then receive extra follow-up, extra tasks and be stimulated to become even better – not like today, when he is pressed down because everyone is going to be the same. If a student is struggling with math, he should be adapted to training and stimulated to learn in his own way — not like today, when many fall outside at an early stage and perhaps never come up again. The Government has presented a proposal for the introduction of a probationary system of practical upper secondary vocational training. We in the Progress Party see that the school is perceived as too theoretical for many, and that more people need a practical approach. In the past, we had a practical subject in high school. We're going to reintroduce that as a permanent arrangement. At the same time, we need to reduce the theoretical proportion in professional directions. ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e1331	Last month I was a guest at the Church Meeting in Tønsberg. The church meeting in 2009 marked an anniversary; it was 25 years since the first church meeting was held. The establishment of the Church Meeting came as a direct result of the discussion of the church system conducted at the beginning of the 1980s. The church meeting was an answer to church reform demands, where the main thing was increased church autonomy. Over the 25 years since the Church Meeting was established, there has been ongoing church reform. The Church’s organisation has found new forms as more and more tasks and decisions have been transferred from political bodies to church-elected councils at central, regional and local level. The establishment of the Church Meeting in 1984, the transfer of the added responsibility of the priests to the Councils of Bishops at the end of the 1980s and the establishment of the Councils of Churches in each municipality which came under the New Church Act of 1996, are the main milestones of this work. When I pull up the main lines from the last 25 years, it's to show that the church reform and renewal work has been in a continuous process. At the same time, at the beginning of a new ageing period, we know that new and major changes have been made to the church system. On 10 April 2008, an agreement was reached between the political parties at Stortinget on how to regulate relations between the State and the Norwegian Church in the future. In the light of the agreement, proposals have now been made for amendments to all the seven constitutional paragraphs dealing today with the state church system. In other words, the implementation of this agreement will represent the most extensive changes in the framework of the state church system since we received the Constitution in 1814. Although we in Norway have a good tradition of working towards compromises, and especially in matters of overriding national importance, we know that there are political divisions in the view of the state church system. The system is understood by many as a guarantee that the Church will be preserved as a broad, open and inclusive people's church. However, many have been concerned about the problem of the closeness of ties between the state and the church, not least in terms of non-discrimination. I believe that in the church agreement we have been able to find good and balanced solutions, which meet the needs of church members for continuity and membership of the Church, while at the same time facilitating the State to continue to pursue an active, supportive policy towards other religious and life-seeking societies. The Church Agreement presupposes a reform of democracy in the Norwegian Church. The follow-up of this is now well under way. The church elections in the fall were the first of the two elections to be held before the Stortinget adopts a final system for elections to the Church Meeting and the Council of Bishops. The elections were carried out at the same time as the major elections and in line with the leaders otherwise included in the political agreement. The results show that the opening up of church elections has increased. Both the electoral system and the information system will now be carefully evaluated, with a view to further testing at the 2011 church elections. I will come back to Stortinget with a case about this. Regardless of the percentage of participants in church elections, the opening up of the Norwegian Church is stable. The church has, at the base of 3,9 million members. That's 80 pst. by the population. The emergence of church acts is high in the Norwegian population. The bands between people and church are strong. If the Norwegian Church is to be preserved and further developed as a church of the people, it depends on the fact that new generations also find their place and membership in the churches. I have noticed that an overall committee refers to the training of faith as an important prerequisite for maintaining a broad and committed people's church. The awareness of this seems to be high at all levels of the Church. The 2010 church budget is 20 million new. kr. I have noticed that the Committee is concerned with the relationship between a nationwide, open church and a pastoral service that is present in all communities. There's a context that I also want to point out. The state is responsible for financing the performance service, and the appropriations for the performance clearly represent the largest proportion of the church budget. Finally, I would like to point out the major recruitment challenges we face in the coming years. We're ready to go in and work with them. I think it's one of the most important things we can do in the future.

It is necessary to gain more knowledge of the impact of climate change on marine industries, fish stocks and marine ecosystems. Climate change also leads to acidification of the sea, and we need to understand its consequences. We also need to increase knowledge of the role of the sea in climate change. Knowledge building in several other areas is also necessary in order to better meet the new ecosystem-based management requirements of the Marine Resource Act. For the further growth of the aquaculture industry, it is essential to find a lasting solution to the salmon problem. Research on salmonillus has therefore been a priority over several years. In 2010, research efforts on salmonnus will be further strengthened, while monitoring and supervision will be strengthened and food monitoring will be strengthened through, inter alia, stronger instruments. improved sanctioning possibilities. Of other key research areas in aquaculture, I would also like to mention escape and feed access and alternative feed sources. Marine bioprospecting is an area in which Norway has good chances of success. In September, the government presented a national strategy for marine bio-prospecting, and its efforts have been reinforced by 7 million. kr. Together with the appropriations entered in the budget of the Ministry of Industry and Trade, almost 50 million are now proposed. kr for marine bioprospection. I would also like to show that the allocation is proposed to be increased by 2 million. to strengthen research on the link between feed in the aquaculture industry and the health effects of seafood, and to propose funds for research on the use of marine algae for bioenergy. It's proposed to be set at 5.5 million. kr for further design and quality assurance of a new ice-going research vessel. I have noted the Committee's wish for greater coordination of future research vessel needs between the Institute of Marine Research and other State actors and for the Marine Research Institute to have a coordinating role. The establishment of the National Toxicity Committee in 2005 has, according to the Marine Research Institute, contributed to a very good cooperation between state operators of research vessels. An agreed plan for the renewal of research vessels has also been developed, with new ice-going research vessels prioritizing. I am therefore very confident that everything will facilitate good coordination between the operation and renewal of State research vessels. In summary, the 2010 budget provides a good basis for continuing strengthening marine research. Investment in research and development is important and efforts in this field form the basis for Norway’s future value creation.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e2353	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee on Budgetary Cooperation and also for the work that has been done. This is the first budget debate for the new Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence, and I believe that the work of the Committee has so far shown that this merger was correct. It is becoming increasingly difficult to see defence, security, foreign and development policies separately. There is broad agreement on many of the major issues of Norwegian foreign and defence policy. For a small country such as Norway, it is important to have consensus around areas that are important for Norwegian interests. That does not mean, however, that political disagreement does not occur, and I will come back to that. The action space for Norwegian foreign policy is often illustrated by the term ‘strategic triangle’, which describes Norway’s location between Europe, Russia and the transatlantic region. The triangle continues to illustrate key issues for Norway. Firstly, Norway’s relationship with Europe is regulated by the EEA Agreement, an agreement that is becoming less and less familiar with and less interested in the EU, an EU that is now bringing into force the Treaty of Lisbon, and transferring power to bodies in which Norway has little influence. Secondly, we are a neighbour of a more self-confident Russia, to which we must maintain a clean and constructive relationship. At the same time, we see that the country has very big internal challenges. Our relationship with Russia is at the forefront of the North, both in terms of challenges, and in terms of opportunities for good cooperation and neighbourhood. Here I would like to commend the Minister of Foreign Affairs for establishing the concept of High North – Low Tension in the international environment. The third side of the triangle is made up of the transatlantic relationship and our alliance in NATO. The alliance is the cornerstone of our security policy. NATO is developing a new strategic concept, and the balance of the alliance will also be discussed. The operation in Afghanistan is a clear example of how the NATO countries bear the burden differently, and there will be a debate on the future NATO will be an alliance in internal and external circles. Just Afghanistan is the place where many of the threads of Norwegian security, foreign, development and defence policies are brought together. We are involved in the international efforts of both civil and military instruments – across the spectrum, from humanitarian aid through long-term development aid and state building to soldiers. The Afghanistan exposure really shows the need to see policy in context. The crash just had an interpellation debate on Afghanistan. I should therefore not say so much about the situation, but nevertheless point out some aspects I believe are serious. President Karzai must now deliver, and after a disappointing election, the demands of Afghan authorities must be strengthened. Getting to this is absolutely crucial. It is also very challenging that the UN, UNAMA, is now weakened in Afghanistan. It weakens the coordination of civil efforts. UNAMA needs to be strengthened. In a more national perspective, there is growing concern from our soldiers in Afghanistan that the principal and watertight shoots between the civil and military efforts provided by Norway operate only on paper here at home and not on the ground in Afghanistan. Nevertheless, the government chooses to stick to this distinction, which means that the total Norwegian effort of nearly DKK 1.8 billion per year is not as effective as it could have been. Previous coordination of efforts and closer cooperation between civil and military contributions are essential. Over a certain period of time, a distinction has emerged between those of us who believe that there is a great imbalance between the tasks of the defence and the budgets, and those who believe that everything is heading towards the most beautiful order. It is serious when reports from the Defence and the National Audit Office show a completely different picture of the situation than the picture The Stortinget is presented through the budget proposal. It is also serious where, on repeated occasions in the form of a comment, a majority of the Committee must stress the importance of giving the Storting a more thorough and specific decision-making basis than at present, and that it is possible for the Storing to follow up and control deviations and developments through the annual budgets. I hope and expect that the Government and the new Council of State will add to another and far more open practice for information about and from the Defence, both to create an informed and realistic debate and to ensure that the decision-making base of the Storing is as correct as possible. This is not least important for the defence itself. I take this up with me the proposal on which the Right and Christian People's Party stands together.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e2666	In these days, many eyes are turned against Scandinavia. The Copenhagen Climate Summit and tomorrow's Peace Prize distribution will contribute to the focus on two of the main foreign policy challenges we face: the fight against climate change and for peace and disarmament. In the context of the distribution of prices, a broad mobilisation of those who will express their expectations of Obama’s disarmament tanks and a high and clear memory of the obligations to be fulfilled is expected. But disarmament obligations do not only apply to peace winners and majorities. We have a responsibility. We have a responsibility to work continuously for disarmament and a world without nuclear weapons, for arms control and for political solutions to conflicts. A year ago, many of the world's leaders were gathered to sign the Convention against Class Weapons after the long and expedient efforts of civil society and several of my party and government colleagues. The Government Declaration sets out new and important objectives for the work on disarmament and peace, the assessment of NATO's first strategy, stricter legislation on arms exports and the international regulation of the uncontrolled proliferation of weapons and ammunition, concrete measures for a nuclear-free world. Peace, poverty reduction and sustainable development are interdependent. Today, a new arms race is being driven by the arms industry and the idea that the fight against terrorism should be won militarily. This stands in the way of solving poverty, food crises and climate issues, and that is precisely why the fight for disarmament and peace is part of the SV’s basic foundation. We are pleased that the Government is raising this topic high in the budget proposal. But disarmament does not happen primarily because governments, parties or experts take their own initiative. Olof Palme, the Swedish Prime Minister who worked for nuclear disarmament all his life, once said: ‘Dearmament can only be achieved by people in many countries expressing their opinion.’ The mobilization in the 1980s led Gorbachev and Reagan to sign a disarmament agreement. The ban on land mines and class weapons has been fought by a popular movement all over the world. The fight for disarmament and against nuclear weapons can only be won by the fact that many people express their opinion loudly and clearly, so that world leaders are forced to listen. A few days ago, a report on women's rights in Afghanistan came from Human Rights Watch. The women's liberation has been a central point of the argument when soldiers have been sent to Afghanistan, but the report states that eight years' presence of Western soldiers has not made life easier for Afghan women. The reason is conservative leaders who want to deny women fundamental rights, says the report. The aim of development policy is to change power structures, to give voices to the deaf – and especially to women. Priority must be given to women's rights and participation. Women in poor countries are at the bottom of the social path. 75 pst. Of the poorest on the globe, women. Women receive approx. 10 pst. of the total salary payments, while they carry out almost 70 pst. of all work on earth. Strengthening the position of women is central to poverty reduction and a prerequisite for sustainable development. Therefore, the Government has prioritised women's rights and equality both through political action and economics. And it's gratifying that there's a broad consensus on the importance of Cape. 168 Women and gender equality to contribute to the implementation of the action plan on women's rights and gender equality in development. SV has repeatedly argued that warfare is not the way to go to improve the situation in Afghanistan, as the report I referred to also shows, and we need to focus on what else can be done in the future. It has been pointed out in the past that we must bet on Parliament. There's a quarter of the representatives of women. We need to strengthen the position of women by getting them to the negotiating table, so that they too become real participants. And we have to make sure that the Department of Women also strengthens financially. It will be able to strengthen their case and provide a better guarantee that the money actually benefits women. The world's poverty is not random. The fight against poverty is a fight to change unfair international power structures. It is about seeing and understanding forces that inhibit and promote development, and designing a development policy that addresses the challenges that create poverty. It is a work where assistance is one of several instruments. Financial support can help to shift power and resources, thus strengthening people's ability to influence their own situation. It is important that the Government continues an offensive development policy and that the percentage of gross national income for development purposes needs to be increased beyond 1 pst. Finally, let me say a few words about the Copenhagen climate summit. Climate change is the greatest threat to humanity. It is currently uncertain whether countries will be able to agree on a legally binding climate agreement. Rich countries such as Norway have to take their historical responsibility for climate change by committing to large national emission reductions, while paying for emissions cuts in poor countries.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e2759	The broad popular movements have shaped a lot of our society. They have helped shape both our defence policy and, in particular, our aid and development policy. If you look first at our defence tradition, it is built on the will to defend – that it is the will to defend the people that is crucial to our defence. The fact that the defense has confidence in people most, and is based on people most, is crucial for the support the defense will receive, both morally and economically. But in order to ensure defence, you always have to change. For both security policy challenges and technological opportunities change. The defense has been through a major shift over several major periods of time - a transition that has been necessary for the defence to be able to solve the tasks we want within the limits of defence, for 2010 almost NOK 35 billion. The basis for our defence is the popular anchoring, the duty of protection and the first-time service. The quality of the defense depends entirely on the recruitment, and our initial service ensures a broad recruitment. International operations can never or will never be the core task of defence. The defence's core task is to secure our country and our neighbourhoods, and to ensure our sovereignty. The international operations are from our point of view also a contribution to ensuring our own security. Norwegian security is best ensured by contributing to peace, stability and favourable international development, thereby reducing the risk of crises, conflict spread, armed conflict and the spread of war. Just as our defence is based on a popular tradition, our aid and development policy has old and proud historical roots: from the counter-cultures that flourished against the powers of the 1800s to the solidarity of the working class. Historical movements continue to have a significant impact on Norwegian policy, and they have been crucial for supporting our strong commitment to development. It's all the thousands of burning souls around in associations, in congregations and all the other places that have been and are the basis of Norwegian development exposure. It's not the elite, not the big capital, it's people and the popular movements that have produced Norwegian development policy. The objective of foreign policy shall and must be to put Norwegian interests first. However, if this consideration is also primarily directed at development policy, I believe that the broad popular upsurge of Norwegian aid can be confused. And without popular outbursts, development policy no longer has any legitimacy. Development policy can make a great deal of sense in getting more integrated in foreign policy, making a total of more money available. But in my view, the main emphasis of Norwegian development policy and the key pillar of the effort must be needs-based. It is in our national interest to strengthen the UN. A small country such as Norway depends on clear international rules and the high legitimacy of the UN. The current climate summit in Copenhagen and the Rome food summit earlier this autumn show that the world is facing major challenges that could lead to more conflicts in the future. Fight for scarce energy resources, water shortages, that over one billion people have too little food; it is a fact that climate change will reinforce the problems we already have in food and water security. Fighting for resources provides the basis for conflict and instability. War and conflict contribute to poverty and human degradation. Strengthening the UN, the institution which has legitimacy through the Security Council to address conflicts, and which, through its other activities, can go along with development-creating measures, is crucial from the point of view of the Centre Party. Norway is among the largest contributors to the UN. Nevertheless, the centre party considers that further increases in contributions may be justified. As a small state, Norway has an additional need for international rules of play as a security against arbitrary use of power — rules of the game that protect the interests of actors and countries that themselves are not powerful. In addition to contributing financially, we believe it is in our own interest to contribute to strengthening UN operations also with Norwegian military contributions when we have room to prioritize resources for this. Our defense has strong support out there among people. There is widespread popular support for the use of funds for development, and support for a strong UN – a UN-led world order – is rooted in Norwegian tradition, in people and in the Government. The budget we are adopting today supports these pillars of Norwegian policy.

It's about fixed and stowed course in the management of Svalbard, and history has shown us that such management, according to these objectives, has been successful. Predictability and continuity of the management of the archipelago are key. As Stortinget will remember, there are three main points in the Svalbard message: Svalbard as one of the world’s best managed wilderness areas – the relationship with tourism and other travel. The role of Svalbard as a platform for Norwegian and international research, knowledge and education. A robust presence on Svalbard – with particular attention to the future prospects of the coal industry. The government presented in October a budget in which it wanted Norway to continue to have a high level of commitment and to devote considerable resources to assistance. In a time of financial crisis, it may be particularly important for countries to continue their engagement. It is demanding for many – we know – but it is necessary. That is why I am pleased that there is a majority here on the Stortinget who believe that we should continue to be a significant contributor. That's good, but the most important thing is that the money we give is spent in the best possible way, that we think focused on the results we want to achieve. That's why I think it's right that we bet on areas where we have great competence and where we can make a difference. While money is spent on health and education – which many people have been concerned about here today – it is also important that we point out some areas where we can contribute extra, such as investing in women and gender equality, peace building, human rights, humanitarian aid, the environment and sustainable development, oil and clean energy, good governance and the fight against corruption. That's what I call priority: picking out areas. The government has been criticized for focusing only on the 1 pst target, that the goal is more important than the content. Setting level targets is mostly about having clear ambitions and being aware of how much we actually spend on our incomes. From being an offensive country, Norway will become a defensive country, and I do not. There are those who believe that there are completely different instruments to assistance to develop in the poorest countries. But there is something else to swallow the whole package and believe that as long as a liberalizes all trade and goes on liberalisation, there is a development. It's not that simple. The right has drawn up a very interesting debate on where we're going to spend the aid money. Nor do I understand the criticism of the representative of Skovholt Gitmark that no one is going to debate. I was in the 'Here and Now' debate with that representative at the latest today, so I do not fully understand that criticism. But what the Right wants is to cut off support for Latin America and Asia and concentrate on Africa. At the same time, they want us not to provide assistance to countries that are not democratically developed enough – waiting for them to be. I have little faith in almost isolating land and leaving them to themselves and asking them to take a phone when everything is on their care. I don't think you get any effect from it. Do you really think that you get a democratic development just by cutting the aid – that then things will happen by themselves? It is really believed that the third of the population living in poverty, for example. Latin America, will it get better or worse? I fear the last. I am in favour of using aid to develop democracy, to combat corruption and to get people out of poverty, and not to stop aid to countries because they are either politically disagreeable with them or because the country has challenges. I think it's a little dangerous way. To the question of spending money on Africa: we are to spend money on Africa. We spend money on Africa – we still want to do it – but leaving other regions is actually the opposite. Finally, Norway’s assistance can make a difference in the world. We can never solve the world's poverty challenges alone, but we can be a significant contributor. I hope that we can continue to keep the debate on the content of aid going – as several parties want – I think that is right in relation to the money we are going to spend and that is for the benefit of the poor in the world.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3299	The claim about the real economic investment and fulfilment of the defence's long-term plan appears to be rather unlikely, as nominal increases are likely to be produced as real and actual increases, increases largely offset by price and salary compensations, which require extensive efficiency and savings measures that are not chosen to be detailed in the proposition. The government's claim to add 100 million. kr to a real strengthening of the defence is not easy to understand. Both the Air Force and the Home Guard have to invest in a fall in actual funds of several tens of millions of dollars. This seems even more confusing when the Army has to save close to 97 million. kr to achieve a ‘actual’ increase of well over 12 mills. kr. Moreover, when it is not considered appropriate to meet the long-term objectives of investment funds already in the first year, this does not create any further confidence in doing so for the rest of the period. The government’s draft budget is a brief and comprehensive overview of how the defence’s operational capability has changed further throughout 2008 and how new material is still not available to remedy this. The frigates lack spare parts and qualified crew. The old MTBs are phased out long before there is any hope of putting new vessels into service. The old transport aircraft are known to have been taken out of service, while only two new ones have been delivered so far. The Ministry insists that this is a temporary situation, but in the opinion of the Right, this is a situation that already appears to be quite prolonged and which is constantly prolonged due to delays, defects in the equipment supplied and not least lack of money. This leads to confusion between operational training and experience and the fact that key personnel often find other jobs — unfortunately outside the Defence Department. Despite this and similar situations, both in the Army and in the Air Force, the budget proposal claims that the main results in most areas are positive. It is claimed that the defence has become more relevant and has increased operational capability, and it states: ‘The defence has consistently had good operational capability for operations abroad and has been prepared to contribute to peace operations nationally.’ It is recognised correctly that high levels of international activity and lower levels of exercise than planned have shown a slight decrease in the capacity of national incident and crisis management. When this is seen in the context of also admitting that the operational preparedness capability of the Brigades is limited due to the issuing of orders and specialists in ongoing operations, cf. Afghanistan, and that some material is missing, that the Home Guard is only partially operational, that the Coast Guard has poor access to helicopters, that the Maritime Defense has reduced its presence, and that there is poor availability of key air defence platforms, is the Ministry of Defence's claim of positive main findings difficult to reconcile with reality. On the contrary, the defence has an operational capability lower than it has been for a very long time. This concerns the Maritime Defense Agency, which has received an increase of almost 70 million. kr, but which, like the Air Force, the Home Guard and the Army, gets to this so-called plus through internal efficiency and saving. In sum, this means that in order to ensure a real increase in the available defence framework, it is expected that the defence industries will save more than 230 million. kr in 2010. In addition, Norway has spent enormous resources on material investments but, as mentioned above, achieves very little enhanced operational capacity. There is reason to believe that in several areas of investment, these too, unfortunately, will go far in time. This has gone beyond operational capability and it has gone beyond an adequate level of practice. Against this background, through its proposal, the Right has chosen to strengthen the defence budget by more than 500 million. kr for the year 2010, precisely in order to strengthen the chapters relating to the defence sectors and the Home Office, which relate to a proper level of practice and training.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3388	First of all, I would like to thank the chairman of the committee for putting a good deal of work into the vote. There is a strong political consensus on the arrangements and the main course for the conversion of defence, which is one of Norway's largest public conversion projects. It was initiated by the Stoltenberg I government in 2001, followed by the Bondevik II government, and is now being continued by the Stoltenberg II government. To a large extent, the Storting agreement on the course has made it possible to succeed in the large conversion handles and to arrive at the solid results that are now available. Today, we have a relevant defence that holds a high international standard. We safeguard our national interests and rights in the neighbourhoods, and we participate in operations on behalf of the international community. We're doing this in an excellent way, with competent crews and modern materials. It would not have been possible for the Defense to solve its tasks in such a good way without the major changes in the arrangement, structure and organisation that have taken place under several governments. For example, this applies to their comments on the Army’s operating budget, a budget that is actually strengthened by 767 million. kr for the period 2006-2009. The government is following up the Government's defence decisions. So we're going to do 500 of the 800 million. kr of the planned increase in appropriations has been implemented by the submitted budget for the following year. When we also take into account the defence's own actions, about one fifth of the long-term financial assumptions remains to be realised at the end of next year. The implementation of the long-term plan is thus well on track. The increase in appropriations and internally released funds are geared towards the migration objectives of the long-term plan and shall include, inter alia,: go to increased manning, increased exercise and training and reception of new material. In addition, the Government has significantly increased the allocation of operations abroad in relation to the long-term plan. Although the Government is so well on track with the implementation of the long-term plan, tight financial management will be needed until the conversion measures are fully effective. This is marked in all defenses, but especially in the Army and the Home Office, where the level of practice is lower than desired. The government decided to reduce the training in Home Affairs this year to put the structural measures in place in the long-term plan. However, the intention has been that practice and training should increase again beyond the period. That's why we've added that approx. 20 pst. the area structure will be trained next year. As shown in the draft budget, the Government has chosen to give priority to the level of operations next year at the expense of the investment level in order to maximise the initial balance of developments over the long term. One of the designs is paradoxically enough that there are too many annual works in the defense in relation to the planning assumptions. This does not mean that in some areas the number of annual works should not be increased, but that we must distribute the annual works so that the right person can get in the right place. The aim is then to strengthen the budget for material investment in 2011 and 2012 and to increase the level of exercise and training. The defence operational budget has thus been substantially strengthened, and it has been substantially strengthened for the second consecutive year with the proposal for the 2010 budget. With today's budget, the operational budget half-way over the long term will actually be above the foreseen level at the end of the long term. On this background, it is surprising that such a large word is used about an alleged negative state of the defence's operating budget and purchasing power. The operating budget is actually proposed to be reinforced by 455 million. kr from this year to next year. The government is proposing an operational budget for next year, which is actually DKK 1.7 billion above the Defence Operations budget in 2005, in the last year of the Bondevik II government. The increased level of operation includes: for the reception of new aeroplanes and vessels, increased exercise and training activities in the Home Guard and maritime defence. In 2009, the average training in the Army will be 20 days. In 2003, the average was around 12 days. Equally important is that the Bondevik II government significantly reduced the army, while Stoltenberg II has built up the crew with about 50 pst. All in all, we are talking about a formidable increase in the army's exercise volume under this government. There has been a lot of discussion lately about the development of costs in defence and the annual wage and price compensation. However, it is known that this compensation has for several years been significantly above the known level of the consumer price index. Thus, the compensation received by the defence sector, after careful calculations in the Ministry of Finance, takes into account the specific cost growth in the defence sector. Part of the compensation of wages and salaries is post-calculated in the light of the salary structure in the coming years, so that the entire compensation is not reflected in the balance of the budget. However, let me be clear that I see many challenges in the ongoing transition. The Government draws the Storting's attention to the fact that technical problems of the Skold class's propulsion system mean that deliveries will be somewhat delayed compared to what the budget proposal provides. I refer you to my own letter to the Grandfather on the matter. I stress that there is still uncertainty about the updated delivery plan. However, for the defence, it is more important to have a well-functioning platform later than to get something that is not fully operational, as soon as possible. The defence has planned to integrate new equipment, including: new target missiles, on ships in 2011 and 2012. It will therefore still take a few years for the new vessels to be fully exploited. It is also important for me to point out that I take the Riksrevision remarks very seriously. Responsible governance is an obvious objective of modern defence. It will be followed up in 2010. So I do not conceal that the transition is challenging, nor that some priorities can be perceived as very demanding. Overall, however, I believe that the draft budget for next year clearly shows that the Government is paying for the defence and is following the objectives adopted by the Stortinget through the examination of the long-term plan. I look forward to continued cooperation on the implementation of this. The escalation of the defence budget continues, and the solid start of the first two years of the long term facilitates the follow-up of the North Sea effort and the provision of robust and requested strengths to operations abroad.
