ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3512	Norway is a small country in a densely interwoven world. We always have to remember that most people live in India and China. Globalization is very positive for us. We are one of the world's winners because of globalisation, not despite it. It has given us access to important markets for our export industry, it gives us greater cultural understanding, and it has given us cheap imports of consumer goods and access to labour through labour, particularly through the EEA – to mention something. The world is in a very serious financial crisis, and the financial crisis also affects foreign policy in several ways. The need for stronger control of financial markets is evident and there is a willingness to do so, including: in the EU. Large budget deficits during the financial crisis in very many countries also lead to cuts in many national budgets. Norway is in many ways an exception. The financial crisis is therefore a very good example of how the global and national are two sides of the same coin. Today, the Nordic regions are Norway’s main strategic focus on foreign policy. There's a lot of reasons for that. Climate change is very visible in the north. Northern areas are likely to have large oil and gas resources and some of the world's richest fishing resources. When the melting of the ice opens up new and shorter international transport routes between the Pacific and the Atlantic, which are too cold for pirates, new challenges and opportunities await in the area. We have an active policy in the northern regions. Both enhanced cooperation with Russia and our Nordic neighbours through Barents cooperation and Arctic cooperation is very important for Norway. Nevertheless, the people who live in the north, and who live in and with resource wealth, are the best of all to see the results of the effort. The Barents region is in reality becoming a single labour market. The future in the north implies increased activity on both the Norwegian and Russian sides. From 3000 border crossings between Norway and Russia in 1990, almost 110 000 cross the Norwegian-Russian border this year. The drivers behind them include: Norwegian enterprises with labour shortages, which will employ Russians, but more Norwegian enterprises are now engaged in Russia. Only in Murmansk, I've heard, over 20 Norwegian companies are established. It is important to have a simpler regulatory framework for border control with Russia in the north. Norway is now negotiating with Russia on a local border traffic regime, which will allow the population of border areas – within 30 km of each side – to cross the border without a visa. I hope we succeed in that. After the government’s Northern Territory Initiative was presented in the previous period, it has been increased by DKK 1.5 billion. In 2010, we are increasing the allocation by new 550 million. kr. It provides room for seven long-term strategic areas involving most ministries. In addition to this, there are several sectoral initiatives, such as: National transport plan and Defence’s long-term plan, which prioritises the northern regions. Perhaps even more than measured in crowns and ears, the political initiative behind the Northern Region Initiative of the Government has produced results. Our position as leading countries in the development of the north is strengthened. It's not obvious, but it's important to us. The understanding of the need for strong Norwegian and international focus and cooperation in the north is established and anchored both nationally, through broad support in the Stortinget, and internationally, through extensive international cooperation, not least through several projects. Both the EU and the US have presented their north-eastern strategies with plans for a stronger commitment. Russia also has such plans. Last night, Council conclusions on Arctic issues were adopted by EU foreign ministers. They conclude here that an EU policy for the Arctic should be based on international maritime law – an important breakthrough for Norway. The Council also opens up to the establishment of an Arctic Information Centre in the EU. The international interest in the Arctic and the northernmost regions of the world will only continue to increase. The government's ambition is to be ahead of this development. This is, of course, a position we in the Labour Party support. To achieve this, work must be part of the daily life of any department. Work must be well coordinated. Study visits and visits to Norwegian and foreign target groups continue to be organised. There's a lot of people who need knowledge about what's going on in the north. This work will be reinforced next year, and reputational work will be strengthened, inter alia. through our foreign stations. It's important. The coastguard oversees vessels and prevents illegal fishing in the north, and has been strengthened in recent years, inter alia. by increasing the number of days sailing. Overfished by cod has been reduced by 60 pst. The phase-in of new frigates will strengthen Norway's military capacity in the north. At the end of the 1990s, Norwegian fighter aircraft were on the wings to follow Russian planes three to four times. Last year, this increased to 87 Russian planes. In the choice of purchase of Norwegian fighter aircraft, the ability to monitor, patrol and exercise sovereignty over large sea areas has been important requirements for the aeroplanes. This also means a great deal in a north-eastern perspective.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3542	As the case rapporteur for the Svalbard budget, I would like to add a few words to that. Svalbard is, of course, a central part of the northern regions for Norway. The management of Svalbard is based on Norway’s full and unrestricted sovereignty over the island group, which is clearly enshrined in the Svalbard Treaty of 1920. The objectives of Norwegian svalbard policy have been established for a long time and form the basis for a stable and predictable exercise of authority for this group of islands. Norway has a significant responsibility for ensuring peace, stability, the protection of the wilderness environment and responsible resource use and management at Svalbard. By virtue of their sovereignty, Norway may also apply to Svalbard all the laws of the mainland as long as the law does not conflict with the provisions of the Svalbard Treaty. One example of this may be that the EEA Agreement does not apply to Svalbard and therefore there are different rules for Svalbard than for mainland Norway. The budget statement shows that Norwegian svalbard policy is largely politically anchored here in Stortinget. It also shows that we're going to go in for a 17.1 million increase in the budget. kr, i.e. 7.4 pst. increase in relation to the 2009 budget. We have previously addressed a report on Svalbard in Stortinget, which also established a broad political consensus on the future. No one visiting Svalbard can avoid being turned off by the powerful wilderness nature of this island group. Svalbard represents a natural heritage and a cultural heritage that Norwegian authorities have a specific responsibility to preserve. One of the main objectives of the svalbard policy is to preserve the unique wilderness nature. The environmental status of Svalbard is essentially very good today. Norway has long traditions of polar research, especially in the Arctic, and is also considered to be a heavy research nation in this field. In recent decades, large resources have been invested in research and education infrastructure, both in Longyearbyen and in New Zealand. This facilitates the strengthening of Svalbard as the international research arena. Further priority for Arctic research is widely supported. A robust presence has been and is an important tool in Norwegian swallowing policy. For a long time, coal has been critical for the Norwegian settlement in Longyearbyen. Nevertheless, coal management is essential for the Longyearbyen as a well-developed family community, and without coal-fired housing would be much less extensive, and likely to vary — at least the number of northern Svalbards applies, as under current rules no restrictions can be imposed on other countries' coal operations and settlements. But even though the coal industry has been and is important, other industries have also taken up their responsibilities. The growth of tourism as a basic industry is reflected in the increase in the number of guest days from barely 20,000 in 1991 to over 86 000 in 2007. Travel life activities in vulnerable areas require both knowledge of the actors and regulation by the authorities. Most of the growth in activity and population on Svalbard can be related to Longyearbyen. The town of Isfjorden has had a significant growth in the population over the past ten years. By 31 December 2008, there were 2,018 residents residing in Longyearbyen. Now, society is facing a capacity limit with regard to local infrastructure. A growth similar to that in the last ten years cannot be expected in the future, and further growth in the population will trigger the need for large investment. The Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence points out that the Arctic landscape is vulnerable, and it is important that this vulnerability be taken into account by further focusing on business development and travel. These considerations need to be balanced against the general desire for stable family housing. A good swallowing policy requires coordinated efforts by several departments. Coordination of these efforts is important, and it is absolutely necessary to make good use of resources in line with the general political and more specific environmental policy objectives. But activity at nearly 80 degrees north puts both people and equipment on a test. This budgetary position shows that an aggregate increase wants to facilitate good governance where development and activity are adapted to the specific challenges facing the Svalbard community in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3721	Nothing in Norwegian foreign policy can be more important than Norway’s relationship with the EU. No political association outside of population and government has a greater influence on Norway’s national policies than the EU. Nevertheless, the importance of the matter is inversely proportional to the attention of the matter in the public debate, and unfortunately also in this Chamber. Therefore, this small contribution to remind you that Norway is part of Europe, although Europe can hardly be said to be part of Norway. We've been outside the most important political community in our part of the world. So we have also said our part of the responsibility for the development of the continent of which we are part. In the name of real policy, it may be justified to sacrifice solidarity on the altar of self-determination, but it requires that we actually have ourselves, not that we pay others to do much of the job. My intention is not to compromise the excellence of the EEA Agreement, we agree on it, but to raise legitimate questions about the adequacy of the agreement. We are in practice a member of the EU, with four exceptions: fish, agriculture, the euro and participation. While many of the exporting industries want euro during these days, there should be a lack of participation of representatives in this room that concerns them. It should be concerned that significant parts of the framework conditions for Norwegian municipalities, Norwegian business, Norwegian judicial policy, Norwegian climate and environmental policy are not determined at Stortinget, but in Brussels. It should be a matter of concern that the Minister of Defence cannot attend meetings setting a framework for the use of Norwegian soldiers in EU operations. It should be concerned that Norway has drawn out its only national expert in the European Parliament, as set out in the only committee that we can be absolutely sure that, for the most part, is the EEA-relevant, namely the Internal Market Committee. It should be concerned that the EU's desire is increasingly to coordinate a common foreign and security policy, which in practice could form a strong block within the NATO Alliance and a clearer voice outside. For a country whose security policy is based on NATO membership and outside Europe, this is not necessarily a good development. Not least, it should be concerned that we still have debates and opinions on important EU directives which have been adopted several years earlier and which have actually sent us more to information than to voting. This is not my concern alone. Norway’s EU ambassador is also concerned about the adequacy of the EEA Agreement to cater for Norwegian interests in the face of a more dynamic and democratic EU. The EEA Agreement is good, but it is not good enough. What we get is the internal market in Europe, and that's a right we don't want to be apart, but what we pay is our democratic right to participate in Norway's development and future in important areas. As this is a budget debate, I would like to point out that it is a rigid price to pay.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3736	My post has a special address for the Minister of Defence. I was expecting the Government in its Declaration of Accession – Soria Moria II – to concretely promote a collection of the ICT services of the Defence at Jørstadmoen, and I quote: « bring together, as far as possible, ICT resources in the Defence in line with the Long-Term Plan and Option S 318 [[...]] [[2007-2008]] Although this is not fully met, the more committed right-wing approach to a full set of ICT services, with the strengthening of ICT work that this would mean, is clear that it is a positive signal that we will follow with interest in the coming years. It is with concern I have noted that the defense itself later to build a parallel organization under the head of INI in Oslo. We had a rather heated debate on this in connection with the previous budget, where the Right's starting point was, and is, that the Government did not follow up what was a unanimous position from the Stortinge in connection with the long-term plan. I leave the question of the government's lack of follow-up in this matter at this crossroads, but must point out that the defence's handling of the matter appears to be a strong disregard for a clearly expressed political will. When the Government now attaches such importance to this matter that it is concrete in Soria Moria II, I take it as a signal that the Government will at least follow up with it, in our opinion, the second best, namely a collection ‘as far as possible’. The question that then arises is, of course, what ‘as far as possible’ really means. The term is rather non-obligatory and requires a deepening. I expect the government itself to have a sense of what this is going to be about, and that the Storting will be informed about what collection it's going to add up to, and at what rate this will happen. I hope the Council of State can comment on this today, if the occasion permits. If not, we'll see to it that there are several crossroads where we can discuss this issue, as far as this is a matter where there will be relatively much attention in the future. This is a matter where the government has a significant need to build trust. It requires clear objectives and political action. In the light of this case’s history, there will also be a political need to have a real insight into the follow-up and organisation of ICT work by the Defence Agency. It must be clear that, on the part of the Great Britain, we cannot accept the fact that we are organising around what is clearly expressed political objectives. The right-hand position is still a full collection of ICT services at Jørstadmoen, and a strengthening of them – and then we see what the Government is doing.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3760	I asked for the floor after hearing the statements by the representatives of Hadia Tajik and Anette Trettebergstien regarding women's rights in some less developed countries. What they are criticising is that the Progress Party has proposed to remove, or significantly reduce, the financial support to some such countries. They wonder about it. What I find strange is that the two actually call for the worst countries to be encouraged by direct gifts of money – or call it budget support – to those regimes that suppress. Instead, the Progress Party will make demands for democratic development and not least demands for progress on equality and women's rights, in the countries concerned, i.e. take money from the rulers. Then the most corrupt dictators weaken, and we can get a little bit further. At least I can't see any examples of dictators getting any better when you give them more money. Several countries — and some of them have been mentioned in the debate today — have long since come to the conclusion that this type of reaction should be made through a strong reduction or even elimination of subsidies to those countries that behave badly with regard to women’s rights and other human rights.

One is Russian flights, which have shown a marked increase in the last three years. Today’s activity, however, is far from the activity of the Cold War. We have no reason to believe that this is specifically addressed to Norway. But we're going to follow it very closely. There is also reason to believe that the Russian defense has plans for training and training its forces and their materials. That it has also been hit by economic decline is probably also a fact, but perhaps to a lesser extent than what the Russian economy in general so far shows. With regard to the practice situation of Home Protection, I shall not say anything else in this round but that conversion, as I have said, requires priorities, and that we have had a smaller activity in the current year, is a fact. We're still adding up to the increased exercise of the forces next year. The so-called reinforcement and exercise strengths will exercise 20 pst., with an increased path towards the end of the long-term plan of 50 pst. Priority is given to intervention forces in this context, as is known. As far as Jørstadmoen is concerned, I would like to say that it is a theme that I have already been challenged on in the Questionnaire, and which I also have a lot of attention around. It is a strong environment on Jørstadmoen, which we will also assess how we can develop further. So it's true that there's a separate head of defence information infrastructure. I can also say this area, with the background of the technology industry, is an area where you have to look at both the virtual organisation – call it it – and the real location. Infrastructure is important to enable development from different locations to be carried out locally. I think Jørstadmoen is very well positioned to do a good job also in the continuation of the area for which they are responsible.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-12-09.ana.ud773e3776	I also have no intention of extending the debate very much, but one of the interesting features of today's debate has been that the Progress Party is now coming in an offensive way and is telling them that they will grant NOK 18 billion for aid or development. This is a significant amount, and, as the Progress Party itself drew up, that is to say, an amount greater than Norway's overall assistance under the Bondevik Government, although the figures are not entirely comparable. It's a very large amount of money. However, in the debate I do not hear a single argument from the Progress Party for this. I hear a continuous series of reasons why no assistance should be given, while the party will give NOK 18 billion. I would just like to recommend that the next time we have a debate on this in Stortinget, the Progress Party should also come up with a good and clear reason why they will spend such a large amount of taxpayers' money for development efforts. The only country in which development is being promoted and defended is Afghanistan. I have a number of proposals for countries where women's liberation has come much further than in Afghanistan, if that is a desirable goal. And if corruption is the central thing, I just have to remind you that Afghanistan scores roughly on top of the list of all international corruption indices. Also there is a series of developing countries that have come much further in the fight against corruption than Afghanistan, if you want to bring something. I am speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats) and European Democrats, and I would like to speak on behalf of the Group of the European United Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Green Left/Nordic Then we also have to hear why the party wants to spend so much, and what the positive arguments in the case are. Then the Progress Party has put forward some proposals that I would like to comment on. The party asks in proposal No. 2 on a ‘blacklist’ of assistance organisations that do not take the issue of corruption seriously. I see no need for such a blacklist. But I want to assure you here and now: if the Advancement Party can demonstrate a single Norwegian development organisation or voluntary organisation that does not take the corruption issue seriously, we will now turn back the cash flow to that organisation. We do not want to spend money on any Norwegian volunteer organisation that does not take this seriously. There is no need for a blacklist, but there is a need at the moment to stop the money from that organisation, and I am asking for help from the Progress Party to find out which organisations it might be. Then, cooperation with countries will be sought to take action to ‘reduce bureaucratic barriers to entrepreneurship, trade and innovation’. I think that is an excellent proposal, but I do not think the solution to it is a bureaucratic group in Norway. I think the solution to this is concrete cooperation with individual countries. Let's look at how we can do it in some of the countries where there is the most nutritional cooperation. Finally, in proposal No. 7 asks the Progress Party ‘Government to develop a comprehensive strategy for the greater involvement of industry in development policy’. This is a proposal that I think is positive. We've long assessed whether we should present a big deal about capital in development cooperation, and then this will be a natural part of it. I must remind you that this government has just put industry's efforts in development policy at the top of the agenda.
